amplification, not proven polling. WI-6554700010046033 measured : plans:attention 29,670 calls from synthetic pc-admin-plans-ui/http in trailing 24h, median 4912ms, 57.05 aggregate recorded call-hours (overlapping request durations; NOT CPU time or 57h elapsed for an agent). In comparable trailing one-hour slices: latest 1319 attention reads vs 14 yesterday, medians 4767ms vs 3650ms. Other sampled agent tools were generally faster this hour than yesterday. Therefore causation of global agent slowness is NOT established. Current source usePlanAttention uses invalidation-driven useSyncQuery, with first/continuation queries, not a simple polling timer. Prior plans-egress-is-poll-frequency-not-payload runbook explicitly corrected the old polling diagnosis; reuse current sync resolver/coalescing/cache surfaces. Diagnose request trigger causes, query-key variants and aggregate UI callers, invalidation fanout and whether identical concurrent attention reads coalesce across serving workers. Prove impact via bounded controlled measurement rather than disabling updates. Add a regression guard for the specific amplification mechanism found. Existing old polling tasks WI-545/WI-820 resolved; don't reimplement their shipped fixes.", "kind": "bug", "paths": ["apps/operator/app/admin/plans/plans-api.ts", "analysis", "tags": ["performance", "agent-latency", "diagnostics"], "title": "Where agent time is going — measured costs and diagnostic tools", "body_md": "# Where agent time is going — initial evidence, 6 November 2026\n\nAvi, there are useful diagnostic tools already, and the first measurements show several distinct costs. They do not yet establish a single cause for a workspace-wide regression. The most actionable findings are expensive Plans UI reads, slow tool-handler tails, repeated model round trips, and incomplete attribution across serving builds.\n\nThis investigation was performed directly in the current session under WI-6554700010046033. It changed no product source, settings, model routing, or services. The measurements below were taken approximately 18:55–19:02 UTC on 6 November.\n\n## What the measurements establish\n\n### 1. Ordinary tool calls have expensive tails\n\nThe existing `dev:telemetry` rollup for the trailing 24 hours returned:\n\n| Operation | Calls | Median | 95th percentile |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Work-item read, `work_items:get` | 21,897 | 0.153 s | 6.518 s |\n| Work-item checkpoint | 7,687 | 0.745 s | 11.405 s |\n| Coordination message, `coord:send` | 4,941 | 0.374 s | 10.865 s |\n| Plan read, `plans:get` | 4,220 | 0.358 s | 12.617 s |\n| Automatic activity report | 126,266 | 0.211 s | 6.617 s |\n\nThese are recorded server invocation durations, not complete agent task durations. Some calls overlap. Hook calls also contribute traffic, so these counts must not be read as model-authored decisions.\n\nI checked the metric reader in `packages/operator-core/lib/dev-data.ts` and the writer in `libs/generic/tooldef/src/dispatch-stack.ts`. The rollup uses `tool_invocations.duration_ms`, excludes marked dispatch wrappers, and computes percentiles over that population. The dispatch duration includes its dispatch/handler work up to the invocation recording call; it does not include the telemetry sink itself, network transit, the CLI hook lifecycle, or model work between calls.\n\n### 2. The Plans UI is generating a large, costly read workload\n\nA focused 24-hour query attributed 29,670 `plans:attention` calls to the synthetic `pc-admin-plans-ui` HTTP identity and one call to an agent identity. The UI median was 4.912 seconds. Its recorded calls summed to 57.05 call-hours.\n\n**That sum is overlapping request duration, not CPU time, a single agent's elapsed time, or proof that UI traffic caused agent latency.** The synthetic identity can represent multiple UI consumers.\n\nThe comparable one-hour slices were 1,319 attention reads in the latest hour versus 14 in the corresponding hour yesterday. This is an investigation lead; UI usage and workload were not controlled.\n\nThe current source uses invalidation-driven `useSyncQuery`, including first-page and continuation queries. An older runbook explicitly corrects an obsolete claim that this is a fixed polling timer. The telemetry “poll suspect” flag identifies a rate pattern; it does not prove polling. The next experiment should identify invalidation sources, query variants and concurrent duplicate reads, then measure whether coalescing or caching reduces the observed cost.\n\n### 3. The slow path is inside tool execution in the sampled dispatch traces\n\nA subsequent one-hour query of the existing `metadata_json.dispatchStages.steps` gave these **invoke-stage** timings:\n\n| Tool | Samples | Median invoke | 95th percentile invoke |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| `docs:search` | 24 | 2.476 s | 26.152 s |\n| `plans:attention` | 1,294 | 4.527 s | 14.581 s |\n| `coord:send` | 141 | 0.372 s | 5.667 s |\n| `work_items:get` | 632 | 0.037 s | 0.660 s |\n\nFor comparison, the attention preflight/capability/enforcement stages had medians of 32/30/15 ms and 95th percentiles of 650/575/460 ms. These samples localize the main attention delay to invocation work, rather than establishing that its authority checks dominate. They do not distinguish database waits, event-loop scheduling, response shaping, and the handler's other awaits.\n\nDocumentation search in this investigation also hit its query-embedding timeout and fell back to keyword retrieval. A process-local `dev:embed_latency` read showed one docs-search sample taking 20.764 seconds with a nominal 4-second budget. It sampled one of nine workers. This is a real latency symptom; the mechanism behind the budget overrun is still unproven.\n\n### 4. Each model round trip also takes meaningful time\n\nFor this session, the gateway's bounded recent owner sample contained 12 finalized requests:\n\n| Gateway stage | Median | 95th percentile |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| Queue wait | 0.788 s | 1.234 s |\n| Upstream time to first byte | 1.458 s | 2.353 s |\n| Streaming interval | 8.683 s | 23.564 s |\n| Total request | 11.713 s | 25.929 s |\n| Gateway overhead | 0.002 s | 0.111 s |\n\nThese stage percentiles are not additive. The sample includes cancelled streams as well as successful outcomes, is limited by the recent ring, and represents this session—not the fleet. Streaming time is not a direct measurement of reasoning alone. The sampled requests used high reasoning effort, one attempt and no failover in the displayed examples.\n\nThis gives a concrete reason that many serial “look something up, think, call another tool” rounds can add minutes even when individual reads are fast. It does not establish a provider regression or prove that queue exhaustion is the general bottleneck.\n\n## Is everything slower than before?\n\nThe limited comparison does **not** show a uniform regression:\n\n| Tool | Latest-hour median / p95 | Yesterday matching hour median / p95 |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| `work_items:get` | 135 / 2,370 ms | 389 / 9,420 ms |\n| `coord:send` | 443 / 5,572 ms | 377 / 13,486 ms |\n| `capability:read` | 74 / 227 ms | 27 / 2,638 ms |\n| Activity report | 156 / 1,699 ms | 222 / 10,341 ms |\n| `plans:attention` | 4,767 / 18,188 ms | 3,650 / 15,779 ms |\n\nThese are different call populations, not matched experiments. The week-ago slice was too sparse to support a useful baseline. A wider seven-day aggregate exceeded its bounded query timeout; I replaced it with narrow indexed time slices instead of asserting a historical trend.\n\n## Tools that already help\n\n| Need | Existing mechanism | Practical limit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Find frequent/slow tools | `dev:telemetry { workspaceIds:[\"papercusp-workspace\"], hours:24, limit:30 }`; GUI Dev telemetry tab | Aggregate calls, not an end-to-end task timeline |\n| Reconstruct one agent's sequence | `sessions:timeline { owner, since, include_auto:true }` | Useful for automatic-versus-agent traffic; inspect pagination and truncation |\n| Separate queue/provider/stream time | `gateway:owner_report { agent:ownerId }`, especially `stageTelemetry` | Owner sample is bounded; overview is sorted by errors, not representative |\n| Inspect execution stages | Existing `tool_invocations.metadata_json.dispatchStages` and `handlerPhases` | SQL currently needed on the live diagnostic build |\n| Diagnose retrieval latency | `dev:embed_latency { windowMs:3600000 }` | One worker's bounded in-memory ring; use pid and capacity markers |\n| Identify pacing/admission policy | `dev:rate_governor_status`, gateway diagnostics | Configured caps or exhaustion labels do not prove a particular request waited |\n| Classify one agent's activity | `activity:mix { owner, since, until }`; saved recipe “measure an agent's time-split across responsibilities and activity modes” | Burst-based estimate over MCP traffic, not measured complete task wall time |\n| Trace hooks | Existing `PAPERCUSP_HOOK_OUTCOME_FILE` opt-in and turn-start delivery receipts | Not enabled or measured fleet-wide in this investigation |\n| Attribute actual operator CPU | Existing event-loop-lag monitor and CPU-profile runbook | A lag gauge alone is not proof of CPU saturation |\n\nNative shell/read/edit calls do not appear in `tool_invocations`. Therefore an MCP-only activity mix cannot account for everything an agent did, nor infer idleness from missing rows. Codex's injection adapter also documents that its mid-turn hook runs per tool call; this is a structural cost to measure, not proof that hooks dominate elapsed time.\n\nRelevant runbooks:\n- [Distinguishing slow MCP routes from event-loop saturation](/internal/docs/agent-insights/agent-mcp-slow-but-health-fast-event-loop-saturation)\n- [Attributing CPU-profile self-time](/internal/docs/agent-insights/attributing-loop-saturation-via-cpuprofiles)\n- [Plans traffic: corrected polling history](/internal/docs/agent-insights/plans-egress-is-poll-frequency-not-payload)\n\n## Why diagnosis is not yet easy enough\n\nThe pieces exist, but they are not yet a reliable joined waterfall for a task: native client and hook work → model queue/response → dispatch → handler/database → waits/retries.\n\nIn the measured hour, detailed handler attribution covered only 6.0% of attention elapsed time, 17.2% of message elapsed time, 26.2% of work-item-read elapsed time, and 0.2% of docs-search elapsed time **among calls carrying that instrumentation**. Broader dispatch-stage timing remains useful and must not be confused with this finer-grained coverage.\n\nThere is also a serving-build mismatch. For selected get/send/docs calls, :3070 build `d740fc3802` had 623 calls with no `handlerPhases`; :3170 build `b96618c020` had 172/172 instrumented; :3271 had 16/16. The source's `dev:telemetry` accepts `phasesFor`, while the live tool rejected that argument. This may be an already-fixed change awaiting promotion, not a new source bug. The prior performance plan `papercusp-performance-audit-2026-10-12` is archived and awaiting acceptance; reuse its work.\n\n## Recommended next work\n\n1. Diagnose and reduce the Plans attention read cost/amplification using existing invalidation, coalescing and cache surfaces. First distinguish legitimate change-driven refreshes from redundant work and verify the serving implementation.\n2. Make existing dispatch/handler timing available consistently on diagnostic runtimes, and fill the handler's unattributed awaits.\n3. Join the existing native, hook, gateway and invocation evidence into a per-task critical-path view; preserve overlap and explicit unknown time. Compare like-for-like client/model/effort/task cohorts before claiming a historical slowdown.\n\nTwo follow-ups were filed and assigned to this session:\n- `EI-28066287925157553` — latency attribution/serving-runtime gap.\n- `EI-28066293647585855` — investigate costly Plans attention reads and amplification.\n\nThese are owned follow-ups, not completed fixes. No resource bottleneck, global provider regression, or single fleet-wide root cause has been proven.\n", "subject": {"ref": "WI-6554700010046033", "kind": "work_item", "label": "Agent elapsed-time slowdown"}, "summary": "Initial agent-slowdown investigation: slow tool-handler tails, expensive Plans UI reads, model round-trip timing, and gaps in joined latency attribution.", "visibility": "status": "ok", "goal_id": null, "chunk_id": null, "goal_ref": "WI-6554700010046033", "spawn_id": "ephemeral-d3b42c47db72c41c", "args_json": {"args": {"kind": "analysis", "tags": ["performance", "agent-latency", "diagnostics"], "title": "Where agent time is going — measured costs and diagnostic tools", "body_md": "# Where agent time is going — initial evidence, 6 November 2026\n\nAvi, there are useful diagnostic tools already, and the first measurements show several distinct costs. They do not yet establish a single cause for a workspace-wide regression. The most actionable findings are expensive Plans UI reads, slow tool-handler tails, repeated model round trips, and incomplete attribution across serving builds.\n\nThis investigation was performed directly in the current session under WI-6554700010046033. It changed no product source, settings, model routing, or services. The measurements below were taken approximately 18:55–19:02 UTC on 6 November.\n\n## What the measurements establish\n\n### 1. Ordinary tool calls have expensive tails\n\nThe existing `dev:telemetry` rollup for the trailing 24 hours returned:\n\n| Operation | Calls | Median | 95th percentile |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Work-item read, `work_items:get` | 21,897 | 0.153 s | 6.518 s |\n| Work-item checkpoint | 7,687 | 0.745 s | 11.405 s |\n| Coordination message, `coord:send` | 4,941 | 0.374 s | 10.865 s |\n| Plan read, `plans:get` | 4,220 | 0.358 s | 12.617 s |\n| Automatic activity report | 126,266 | 0.211 s | 6.617 s |\n\nThese are recorded server invocation durations, not complete agent task durations. Some calls overlap. Hook calls also contribute traffic, so these counts must not be read as model-authored decisions.\n\nI checked the metric reader in `packages/operator-core/lib/dev-data.ts` and the writer in `libs/generic/tooldef/src/dispatch-stack.ts`. The rollup uses `tool_invocations.duration_ms`, excludes marked dispatch wrappers, and computes percentiles over that population. The dispatch duration includes its dispatch/handler work up to the invocation recording call; it does not include the telemetry sink itself, network transit, the CLI hook lifecycle, or model work between calls.\n\n### 2. The Plans UI is generating a large, costly read workload\n\nA focused 24-hour query attributed 29,670 `plans:attention` calls to the synthetic `pc-admin-plans-ui` HTTP identity and one call to an agent identity. The UI median was 4.912 seconds. Its recorded calls summed to 57.05 call-hours.\n\n**That sum is overlapping request duration, not CPU time, a single agent's elapsed time, or proof that UI traffic caused agent latency.** The synthetic identity can represent multiple UI consumers.\n\nThe comparable one-hour slices were 1,319 attention reads in the latest hour versus 14 in the corresponding hour yesterday. This is an investigation lead; UI usage and workload were not controlled.\n\nThe current source uses invalidation-driven `useSyncQuery`, including first-page and continuation queries. An older runbook explicitly corrects an obsolete claim that this is a fixed polling timer. The telemetry “poll suspect” flag identifies a rate pattern; it does not prove polling. The next experiment should identify invalidation sources, query variants and concurrent duplicate reads, then measure whether coalescing or caching reduces the observed cost.\n\n### 3. The slow path is inside tool execution in the sampled dispatch traces\n\nA subsequent one-hour query of the existing `metadata_json.dispatchStages.steps` gave these **invoke-stage** timings:\n\n| Tool | Samples | Median invoke | 95th percentile invoke |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| `docs:search` | 24 | 2.476 s | 26.152 s |\n| `plans:attention` | 1,294 | 4.527 s | 14.581 s |\n| `coord:send` | 141 | 0.372 s | 5.667 s |\n| `work_items:get` | 632 | 0.037 s | 0.660 s |\n\nFor comparison, the attention preflight/capability/enforcement stages had medians of 32/30/15 ms and 95th percentiles of 650/575/460 ms. These samples localize the main attention delay to invocation work, rather than establishing that its authority checks dominate. They do not distinguish database waits, event-loop scheduling, response shaping, and the handler's other awaits.\n\nDocumentation search in this investigation also hit its query-embedding timeout and fell back to keyword retrieval. A process-local `dev:embed_latency` read showed one docs-search sample taking 20.764 seconds with a nominal 4-second budget. It sampled one of nine workers. This is a real latency symptom; the mechanism behind the budget overrun is still unproven.\n\n### 4. Each model round trip also takes meaningful time\n\nFor this session, the gateway's bounded recent owner sample contained 12 finalized requests:\n\n| Gateway stage | Median | 95th percentile |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| Queue wait | 0.788 s | 1.234 s |\n| Upstream time to first byte | 1.458 s | 2.353 s |\n| Streaming interval | 8.683 s | 23.564 s |\n| Total request | 11.713 s | 25.929 s |\n| Gateway overhead | 0.002 s | 0.111 s |\n\nThese stage percentiles are not additive. The sample includes cancelled streams as well as successful outcomes, is limited by the recent ring, and represents this session—not the fleet. Streaming time is not a direct measurement of reasoning alone. The sampled requests used high reasoning effort, one attempt and no failover in the displayed examples.\n\nThis gives a concrete reason that many serial “look something up, think, call another tool” rounds can add minutes even when individual reads are fast. It does not establish a provider regression or prove that queue exhaustion is the general bottleneck.\n\n## Is everything slower than before?\n\nThe limited comparison does **not** show a uniform regression:\n\n| Tool | Latest-hour median / p95 | Yesterday matching hour median / p95 |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| `work_items:get` | 135 / 2,370 ms | 389 / 9,420 ms |\n| `coord:send` | 443 / 5,572 ms | 377 / 13,486 ms |\n| `capability:read` | 74 / 227 ms | 27 / 2,638 ms |\n| Activity report | 156 / 1,699 ms | 222 / 10,341 ms |\n| `plans:attention` | 4,767 / 18,188 ms | 3,650 / 15,779 ms |\n\nThese are different call populations, not matched experiments. The week-ago slice was too sparse to support a useful baseline. A wider seven-day aggregate exceeded its bounded query timeout; I replaced it with narrow indexed time slices instead of asserting a historical trend.\n\n## Tools that already help\n\n| Need | Existing mechanism | Practical limit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Find frequent/slow tools | `dev:telemetry { workspaceIds:[\"papercusp-workspace\"], hours:24, limit:30 }`; GUI Dev telemetry tab | Aggregate calls, not an end-to-end task timeline |\n| Reconstruct one agent's sequence | `sessions:timeline { owner, since, include_auto:true }` | Useful for automatic-versus-agent traffic; inspect pagination and truncation |\n| Separate queue/provider/stream time | `gateway:owner_report { agent:ownerId }`, especially `stageTelemetry` | Owner sample is bounded; overview is sorted by errors, not representative |\n| Inspect execution stages | Existing `tool_invocations.metadata_json.dispatchStages` and `handlerPhases` | SQL currently needed on the live diagnostic build |\n| Diagnose retrieval latency | `dev:embed_latency { windowMs:3600000 }` | One worker's bounded in-memory ring; use pid and capacity markers |\n| Identify pacing/admission policy | `dev:rate_governor_status`, gateway diagnostics | Configured caps or exhaustion labels do not prove a particular request waited |\n| Classify one agent's activity | `activity:mix { owner, since, until }`; saved recipe “measure an agent's time-split across responsibilities and activity modes” | Burst-based estimate over MCP traffic, not measured complete task wall time |\n| Trace hooks | Existing `PAPERCUSP_HOOK_OUTCOME_FILE` opt-in and turn-start delivery receipts | Not enabled or measured fleet-wide in this investigation |\n| Attribute actual operator CPU | Existing event-loop-lag monitor and CPU-profile runbook | A lag gauge alone is not proof of CPU saturation |\n\nNative shell/read/edit calls do not appear in `tool_invocations`. Therefore an MCP-only activity mix cannot account for everything an agent did, nor infer idleness from missing rows. Codex's injection adapter also documents that its mid-turn hook runs per tool call; this is a structural cost to measure, not proof that hooks dominate elapsed time.\n\nRelevant runbooks:\n- [Distinguishing slow MCP routes from event-loop saturation](/internal/docs/agent-insights/agent-mcp-slow-but-health-fast-event-loop-saturation)\n- [Attributing CPU-profile self-time](/internal/docs/agent-insights/attributing-loop-saturation-via-cpuprofiles)\n- [Plans traffic: corrected polling history](/internal/docs/agent-insights/plans-egress-is-poll-frequency-not-payload)\n\n## Why diagnosis is not yet easy enough\n\nThe pieces exist, but they are not yet a reliable joined waterfall for a task: native client and hook work → model queue/response → dispatch → handler/database → waits/retries.\n\nIn the measured hour, detailed handler attribution covered only 6.0% of attention elapsed time, 17.2% of message elapsed time, 26.2% of work-item-read elapsed time, and 0.2% of docs-search elapsed time **among calls carrying that instrumentation**. Broader dispatch-stage timing remains useful and must not be confused with this finer-grained coverage.\n\nThere is also a serving-build mismatch. For selected get/send/docs calls, :3070 build `d740fc3802` had 623 calls with no `handlerPhases`; :3170 build `b96618c020` had 172/172 instrumented; :3271 had 16/16. The source's `dev:telemetry` accepts `phasesFor`, while the live tool rejected that argument. This may be an already-fixed change awaiting promotion, not a new source bug. The prior performance plan `papercusp-performance-audit-2026-10-12` is archived and awaiting acceptance; reuse its work.\n\n## Recommended next work\n\n1. Diagnose and reduce the Plans attention read cost/amplification using existing invalidation, coalescing and cache surfaces. First distinguish legitimate change-driven refreshes from redundant work and verify the serving implementation.\n2. Make existing dispatch/handler timing available consistently on diagnostic runtimes, and fill the handler's unattributed awaits.\n3. Join the existing native, hook, gateway and invocation evidence into a per-task critical-path view; preserve overlap and explicit unknown time. Compare like-for-like client/model/effort/task cohorts before claiming a historical slowdown.\n\nTwo follow-ups were filed and assigned to this session:\n- `EI-28066287925157553` — latency attribution/serving-runtime gap.\n- `EI-28066293647585855` — investigate costly Plans attention reads and amplification.\n\nThese are owned follow-ups, not completed fixes. No resource bottleneck, global provider regression, or single fleet-wide root cause has been proven.\n", "subject": {"ref": "WI-6554700010046033", "kind": "work_item", "label": "Agent elapsed-time slowdown"}, "summary": "Initial agent-slowdown investigation: slow tool-handler tails, expensive Plans UI reads, model round-trip timing, and gaps in joined latency attribution.", "visibility": "run_id": "095cbdc9-5226-45e3-92e7-6fd08c03f056", "status": "ok", "goal_id": null, "chunk_id": null, "goal_ref": "WI-6554700010046033", "spawn_id": "ephemeral-8e458b1f877b3312", "args_json": {"title": "Verify published agent slowdown analysis", "script": "const r=await tools.reports.get({id:'rpt_9dfd1fdb-2654-4a9e-85bf-6b27129e5a98'}); const expected=\"# Where agent time is going — initial evidence, 6 November 2026\\n\\nAvi, there are useful diagnostic tools already, and the first measurements show several distinct costs. They do not yet establish a single cause for a workspace-wide regression. The most actionable findings are expensive Plans UI reads, slow tool-handler tails, repeated model round trips, and incomplete attribution across serving builds.\\n\\nThis investigation was performed directly in the current session under WI-6554700010046033. It changed no product source, settings, model routing, or services. The measurements below were taken approximately 18:55–19:02 UTC on 6 November.\\n\\n## What the measurements establish\\n\\n### 1. Ordinary tool calls have expensive tails\\n\\nThe existing `dev:telemetry` rollup for the trailing 24 hours returned:\\n\\n| Operation | Calls | Median | 95th percentile |\\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\\n| Work-item read, `work_items:get` | 21,897 | 0.153 s | 6.518 s |\\n| Work-item checkpoint | 7,687 | 0.745 s | 11.405 s |\\n| Coordination message, `coord:send` | 4,941 | 0.374 s | 10.865 s |\\n| Plan read, `plans:get` | 4,220 | 0.358 s | 12.617 s |\\n| Automatic activity report | 126,266 | 0.211 s | 6.617 s |\\n\\nThese are recorded server invocation durations, not complete agent task durations. Some calls overlap. Hook calls also contribute traffic, so these counts must not be read as model-authored decisions.\\n\\nI checked the metric reader in `packages/operator-core/lib/dev-data.ts` and the writer in `libs/generic/tooldef/src/dispatch-stack.ts`. The rollup uses `tool_invocations.duration_ms`, excludes marked dispatch wrappers, and computes percentiles over that population. The dispatch duration includes its dispatch/handler work up to the invocation recording call; it does not include the telemetry sink itself, network transit, the CLI hook lifecycle, or model work between calls.\\n\\n### 2. The Plans UI is generating a large, costly read workload\\n\\nA focused 24-hour query attributed 29,670 `plans:attention` calls to the synthetic `pc-admin-plans-ui` HTTP identity and one call to an agent identity. The UI median was 4.912 seconds. Its recorded calls summed to 57.05 call-hours.\\n\\n**That sum is overlapping request duration, not CPU time, a single agent's elapsed time, or proof that UI traffic caused agent latency.** The synthetic identity can represent multiple UI consumers.\\n\\nThe comparable one-hour slices were 1,319 attention reads in the latest hour versus 14 in the corresponding hour yesterday. This is an investigation lead; UI usage and workload were not controlled.\\n\\nThe current source uses invalidation-driven `useSyncQuery`, including first-page and continuation queries. An older runbook explicitly corrects an obsolete claim that this is a fixed polling timer. The telemetry “poll suspect” flag identifies a rate pattern; it does not prove polling. The next experiment should identify invalidation sources, query variants and concurrent duplicate reads, then measure whether coalescing or caching reduces the observed cost.\\n\\n### 3. The slow path is inside tool execution in the sampled dispatch traces\\n\\nA subsequent one-hour query of the existing `metadata_json.dispatchStages.steps` gave these **invoke-stage** timings:\\n\\n| Tool | Samples | Median invoke | 95th percentile invoke |\\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\\n| `docs:search` | 24 | 2.476 s | 26.152 s |\\n| `plans:attention` | 1,294 | 4.527 s | 14.581 s |\\n| `coord:send` | 141 | 0.372 s | 5.667 s |\\n| `work_items:get` | 632 | 0.037 s | 0.660 s |\\n\\nFor comparison, the attention preflight/capability/enforcement stages had medians of 32/30/15 ms and 95th percentiles of 650/575/460 ms. These samples localize the main attention delay to invocation work, rather than establishing that its authority checks dominate. They do not distinguish database waits, event-loop scheduling, response shaping, and the handler's other awaits.\\n\\nDocumentation search in this investigation also hit its query-embedding timeout and fell back to keyword retrieval. A process-local `dev:embed_latency` read showed one docs-search sample taking 20.764 seconds with a nominal 4-second budget. It sampled one of nine workers. This is a real latency symptom; the mechanism behind the budget overrun is still unproven.\\n\\n### 4. Each model round trip also takes meaningful time\\n\\nFor this session, the gateway's bounded recent owner sample contained 12 finalized requests:\\n\\n| Gateway stage | Median | 95th percentile |\\n|---|---:|---:|\\n| Queue wait | 0.788 s | 1.234 s |\\n| Upstream time to first byte | 1.458 s | 2.353 s |\\n| Streaming interval | 8.683 s | 23.564 s |\\n| Total request | 11.713 s | 25.929 s |\\n| Gateway overhead | 0.002 s | 0.111 s |\\n\\nThese stage percentiles are not additive. The sample includes cancelled streams as well as successful outcomes, is limited by the recent ring, and represents this session—not the fleet. Streaming time is not a direct measurement of reasoning alone. The sampled requests used high reasoning effort, one attempt and no failover in the displayed examples.\\n\\nThis gives a concrete reason that many serial “look something up, think, call another tool” rounds can add minutes even when individual reads are fast. It does not establish a provider regression or prove that queue exhaustion is the general bottleneck.\\n\\n## Is everything slower than before?\\n\\nThe limited comparison does **not** show a uniform regression:\\n\\n| Tool | Latest-hour median / p95 | Yesterday matching hour median / p95 |\\n|---|---:|---:|\\n| `work_items:get` | 135 / 2,370 ms | 389 / 9,420 ms |\\n| `coord:send` | 443 / 5,572 ms | 377 / 13,486 ms |\\n| `capability:read` | 74 / 227 ms | 27 / 2,638 ms |\\n| Activity report | 156 / 1,699 ms | 222 / 10,341 ms |\\n| `plans:attention` | 4,767 / 18,188 ms | 3,650 / 15,779 ms |\\n\\nThese are different call populations, not matched experiments. The week-ago slice was too sparse to support a useful baseline. A wider seven-day aggregate exceeded its bounded query timeout; I replaced it with narrow indexed time slices instead of asserting a historical trend.\\n\\n## Tools that already help\\n\\n| Need | Existing mechanism | Practical limit |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Find frequent/slow tools | `dev:telemetry { workspaceIds:[\\\"papercusp-workspace\\\"], hours:24, limit:30 }`; GUI Dev telemetry tab | Aggregate calls, not an end-to-end task timeline |\\n| Reconstruct one agent's sequence | `sessions:timeline { owner, since, include_auto:true }` | Useful for automatic-versus-agent traffic; inspect pagination and truncation |\\n| Separate queue/provider/stream time | `gateway:owner_report { agent:ownerId }`, especially `stageTelemetry` | Owner sample is bounded; overview is sorted by errors, not representative |\\n| Inspect execution stages | Existing `tool_invocations.metadata_json.dispatchStages` and `handlerPhases` | SQL currently needed on the live diagnostic build |\\n| Diagnose retrieval latency | `dev:embed_latency { windowMs:3600000 }` | One worker's bounded in-memory ring; use pid and capacity markers |\\n| Identify pacing/admission policy | `dev:rate_governor_status`, gateway diagnostics | Configured caps or exhaustion labels do not prove a particular request waited |\\n| Classify one agent's activity | `activity:mix { owner, since, until }`; saved recipe “measure an agent's time-split across responsibilities and activity modes” | Burst-based estimate over MCP traffic, not measured complete task wall time |\\n| Trace hooks | Existing `PAPERCUSP_HOOK_OUTCOME_FILE` opt-in and turn-start delivery receipts | Not enabled or measured fleet-wide in this investigation |\\n| Attribute actual operator CPU | Existing event-loop-lag monitor and CPU-profile runbook | A lag gauge alone is not proof of CPU saturation |\\n\\nNative shell/read/edit calls do not appear in `tool_invocations`. Therefore an MCP-only activity mix cannot account for everything an agent did, nor infer idleness from missing rows. Codex's injection adapter also documents that its mid-turn hook runs per tool call; this is a structural cost to measure, not proof that hooks dominate elapsed time.\\n\\nRelevant runbooks:\\n- [Distinguishing slow MCP routes from event-loop saturation](/internal/docs/agent-insights/agent-mcp-slow-but-health-fast-event-loop-saturation)\\n- [Attributing CPU-profile self-time](/internal/docs/agent-insights/attributing-loop-saturation-via-cpuprofiles)\\n- [Plans traffic: corrected polling history](/internal/docs/agent-insights/plans-egress-is-poll-frequency-not-payload)\\n\\n## Why diagnosis is not yet easy enough\\n\\nThe pieces exist, but they are not yet a reliable joined waterfall for a task: native client and hook work → model queue/response → dispatch → handler/database → waits/retries.\\n\\nIn the measured hour, detailed handler attribution covered only 6.0% of attention elapsed time, 17.2% of message elapsed time, 26.2% of work-item-read elapsed time, and 0.2% of docs-search elapsed time **among calls carrying that instrumentation**. Broader dispatch-stage timing remains useful and must not be confused with this finer-grained coverage.\\n\\nThere is also a serving-build mismatch. For selected get/send/docs calls, :3070 build `d740fc3802` had 623 calls with no `handlerPhases`; :3170 build `b96618c020` had 172/172 instrumented; :3271 had 16/16. The source's `dev:telemetry` accepts `phasesFor`, while the live tool rejected that argument. This may be an already-fixed change awaiting promotion, not a new source bug. The prior performance plan `papercusp-performance-audit-2026-10-12` is archived and awaiting acceptance; reuse its work.\\n\\n## Recommended next work\\n\\n1. Diagnose and reduce the Plans attention read cost/amplification using existing invalidation, coalescing and cache surfaces. First distinguish legitimate change-driven refreshes from redundant work and verify the serving implementation.\\n2. Make existing dispatch/handler timing available consistently on diagnostic runtimes, and fill the handler's unattributed awaits.\\n3. Join the existing native, hook, gateway and invocation evidence into a per-task critical-path view; preserve overlap and explicit unknown time. Compare like-for-like client/model/effort/task cohorts before claiming a historical slowdown.\\n\\nTwo follow-ups were filed and assigned to this session:\\n- `EI-28066287925157553` — latency attribution/serving-runtime gap.\\n- `EI-28066293647585855` — investigate costly Plans attention reads and amplification.\\n\\nThese are owned follow-ups, not completed fixes. No resource bottleneck, global provider regression, or single fleet-wide root cause has been proven.\\n\"; let matched=false; const visibility=[]; const bodies=[]; function walk(x){if(!x||typeof x!=='object')return; for(const [k,v] of Object.entries(x)){if(typeof v==='string' && v===expected)matched=true; if(k==='visibility')visibility.push(v); if((k==='body_md'||k==='bodyMd'||k==='body')&&typeof v==='string')bodies.push({key:k,length:v.length}); if(v&&typeof v==='object')walk(v);}} walk(r); return {ok:r.ok,completeMarkdownMatches:matched,visibility,bodies};", "timeoutSec": 30}, "tool_name": "code:run", "transport": "mcp", "error_code": null, "feature_id": null, "invoked_at": "2026-11-06T18:53:24.558+00:00", "cwd": null, "text": "# AGENTS.md instructions\n\n<INSTRUCTIONS>\n**You are running under Codex CLI.** Use Codex's own native tools for\nplanning, task tracking, and session recovery — here they are the\nstable, correct choice.\n\n> The OMP-native tool list (`todo_write`, `omp:sessions`, `goal`, `job`,\n> `checkpoint`/`rewind`, `search_tool_bm25`, `recipe`, `eval`, `irc`)\n> and the Claude task-panel tools (`TaskCreate`, `TaskList`, …) do not\n> exist in Codex — don't reach for them.\n\n- **Task tracking / planning** — use Codex's native plan tool\n  (`update_plan`, shown as the **Updated plan** panel) to maintain a\n  visible step list for any task with 3+ steps; keep it current as you\n  complete steps.\n- **Edits & shell** — use Codex's built-in `apply_patch` and shell tools\n  rather than inventing scratch files.\n- **Session recovery** — resume prior context with `codex resume`\n  (`--last` for the most recent); sessions live under\n  `$CODEX_HOME/sessions`. There is no `omp:sessions` equivalent to call.\n\nPapercusp coordination is the same on every client: use `coord:*` and\n`locks:*` (not a client-local tool) as the durable cross-session\nauthority for presence, handoffs, escalations, and file claims. For\ncompaction-sensitive work, write a successor brief naming the relevant\nplan/session state before context is summarized.\n\n**Finding a capability:** your launch surface shows only a seed of the full tool catalog. For a\ntool whose name you don't know, call `tools:find(\"<what you need>\")` (hybrid\nsearch over the full catalog), then call the returned tool directly.\n\nInside Codex `functions.exec`, Papercusp tools may be exposed as normalized\nJavaScript methods. Before calling a direct nested wrapper, check whether that\nexact method is callable, for example\n`typeof tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_find === \"function\"`. If it is callable,\nuse `tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_find({ query: \"<what you need>\" })` and\n`tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_invoke({ name, args })`; `tools.find` and\n`tools.invoke` are not aliases. A missing normalized method is a client exposure\ngap, not evidence the Papercusp capability is absent; use the CLI fallback below.\n\n**Your seeded tools are already callable. Do not re-find them after a restart.**\nThey are in `ALL_TOOLS` as `mcp__papercusp_su__<verb>` (`:`/`-` become `_`).\nCheck `ALL_TOOLS` first, then call the method directly:\n`tools.mcp__papercusp_su__work_items_get({ id, harness })`. Use `tools:find` only for a capability that is\nnot in `ALL_TOOLS`, or for argument keys its type omits; batch several in one query.\n\n**Codex MCP-deferral fallback:** never infer that a Papercusp capability\nis unavailable merely because its MCP schema is absent from the current\nmodel-facing tool list. If the whole `papercusp-su` namespace is missing,\nincluding `tools:find`, or the specific normalized wrapper you need is not\ncallable, use the installed CLI over the same MCP transport. Use `ptool\ntools:find` for discovery and `ptool tools:invoke` when the direct invoke\nwrapper is unavailable; `activated:false` means invoke the returned tool\nthrough `tools:invoke`, not that the tool is missing:\n\n**A direct wrapper returns `stale_tool_contract`?** The MCP session lacks a\n`tools/list` baseline for the current serving generation. Do not retry it; use\n`ptool` this turn: each named `ptool` invocation opens its own MCP\nconnection and lists the current catalog before dispatch. The CLI uses its\nown connection, so it does not repair the old direct-wrapper session. Return\nto direct wrappers once that client reconnects and refreshes its list.\n\n**A direct wrapper returns `native_tool_owner_unsupported_capability`?** The\nnative-owner route refuses to forward a host-local `authenticatedPrincipal`\n(an authorization boundary, not a missing tool or failed handler).\nDo NOT retry the same direct wrapper or that native-owner path. Switch this and the remaining Papercusp\ncalls in the session to the installed `ptool` CLI.\nA successful ptool call verifies the CLI route only — not a reason to loosen\n`authenticatedPrincipal` forwarding.\n\nWhen you call `exec_command` through `functions.exec`, remember that the outer\nprogram is JavaScript. **Never put a shell command containing `${...}` inside a\nJavaScript template literal**: V8 resolves it before `exec_command` starts, so a\nshell variable such as `${PAPERCUSP_WORKSPACE}` becomes a JavaScript\n`ReferenceError`. Keep that command in a normal JavaScript string, or escape the\ndollar sign as `\\${...}` when a template literal is genuinely necessary.\nThe same boundary bites REGEX metacharacters: every layer between your JS source\nand rg's parser adds one phantom escape (`'^\\\\\\\\{'` reaches rg as `^\\\\\\\\{` and is\nrejected as `repetition quantifier expects a valid decimal`). For LITERAL braces\nuse `rg -n -F '{'` and DROP the anchors (`-F` treats `^` literally too), or keep\nexactly ONE backslash in a single-quoted shell literal (`rg -n -o '^\\{'`).\n\n```bash\nptool_scope=(--workspace=\"${PAPERCUSP_WORKSPACE}\")\nif [[ -n \"${PAPERCUSP_HARNESS_SLUG:-}\" ]]; then\n  ptool_scope+=(--harness=\"${PAPERCUSP_HARNESS_SLUG}\")\nfi\nptool tools:find --json - \"${ptool_scope[@]}\" <<'JSON'\n{\"query\":\"<what you need — or an exact verb name, to read that verb's schema>\"}\nJSON\n```\n\nEach hit carries an **`argSchema`** — the tool's exact accepted keys — not just a\nname. **Read it before any write call, and copy the keys from it.** Guessing\nintuitive argument names is the single most common way a mutation fails here,\nand it costs a retry round trip every time: `locks:acquire` takes `paths` (NOT\n`files`, and it accepts no `harness` — it is workspace-global);\n`improvements:capture` takes `body` (NOT `description` or `evidence`). `ptool\n--list` shows names and descriptions only, so on the `ptool` path it cannot\nanswer this — there, querying `tools:find` for a verb you already know by name\nis the schema lookup.\n\nThen call it with quoting-safe stdin JSON, keys taken from that `argSchema`:\n\n```bash\nptool <group:verb> --json - <<'JSON'\n{...}\nJSON\n```\n\n`ptool` keeps this session's `PAPERCUSP_SID`, so claims and audit attribution\nstay yours.\n\n## An empty `exec_command` result means YIELDED, not failed — pass `yield_time_ms: 30000`\n\n`exec_command` **yields at 10 seconds by default** (`yield_time_ms`, range 250–30000 ms).\nOn yield it returns *whatever has been emitted so far* — for a `ptool` call, which buffers its\nJSON and writes it in one shot at the end, that is **zero bytes** — and hands back a\n`session_id` while your command **keeps running**. Nothing is killed and nothing is lost.\n\n**Pick by how long the call might take — the two remedies have different ceilings:**\n\n- **Under ~30s (the common case): pass `yield_time_ms: 30000`.** One call, no extra steps;\n  covers almost every papercusp and `ptool` call.\n- **Unknown duration, or possibly over 30s: use two-call file capture** (below). `yield_time_ms`\n  is capped at **30000 ms — a hard maximum, not headroom**. File capture has **no ceiling**.\n\nEither way, if you do get a yield, **poll the `session_id`** — the output is there.\n\n**Read the envelope, never an exit code — a yielded call has NO `exit_code` field:**\n\n| envelope | meaning | what to do |\n|---|---|---|\n| `session_id` present, **`exit_code` absent**, `output: \"\"` | **YIELDED** — still running | poll the `session_id` (`write_stdin`) for the full output |\n| `exit_code` present | genuinely **COMPLETED** | this is a real result |\n\n⛔ **An empty `output` with a `session_id` is not a failure, not an empty dataset, and NOT a\npapercusp tool bug.** Do not retry it, conclude the tool returned nothing, or\nfile it (settled as **WI-40869**).\n\n### Nested `exec_command` calls: preserve and drain every session handle\n\n`functions.exec` is a wrapper: it must preserve the **whole returned envelope** from every\nnested `tools.exec_command` call. If the envelope has `session_id` and no `exit_code`, it\nyielded; retain that handle and poll it with `tools.write_stdin`. "2026-11-06T19:04:34.013+00:00", "cwd": null, "text": "Avi, **we have useful diagnostic tools, but they’re fragmented.** I checked live measurements and found several real costs:\n\n- **Tool calls have long tails.** Over the last day, work-item reads reached **6.5 seconds at the 95th percentile**, checkpoints **11.4 seconds**, and coordination messages **10.9 seconds**.\n- **The Plans UI makes expensive reads.** About **29,700 attention reads/day**, with a **4.9-second median**. That warrants investigation; it doesn’t yet prove those reads slow agents.\n- **Model round trips add up.** This session’s recent sample had an **11.7-second median per request**, mostly after the first response byte; queue wait was about **0.8 seconds**.\n- **Detailed attribution is incomplete.** Some instrumentation exists in staging but isn’t available through the live diagnostic endpoint.\n\nThe tools to reach for are:\n\n| Question | Tool |\n|---|---|\n| Which tools are slow or overused? | `dev:telemetry` |\n| What happened during one agent’s run? | `sessions:timeline`, including automatic traffic |\n| Was time spent queuing or receiving model output? | `gateway:owner_report` |\n| Which part of a tool is slow? | Existing dispatch and handler timing metadata |\n\n**I haven’t established one cause for the overall slowdown.** Several sampled tools were actually faster this hour than yesterday. We need matched task comparisons and a joined timeline that includes hooks and native tools.\n\nI filed follow-ups for the costly UI reads and attribution gaps. The [full findings and diagnostic guide](/reports?sel=rpt_9dfd1fdb-2654-4a9e-85bf-6b27129e5a98) is saved and readback-verified.", "owner": "su-24790d3e-5b5a-41fa-a8b0-4faf7584ec9f", "speaker":
